     ~BTokenLinkTarget~ we use the target, but also inject a special [[link-marker]]
     which uses an ASCII ~NUL~ character.

  2) Split the contents by newline.

  3) Group together the lines that have Noweb links. In other words, consecutive
     lines that have Noweb links are grouped together.

     For a line to be considered for this group, it must end with a Noweb link;
     if that link is followed by any text, we leave it alone. This is because we
     only ever want to add newline padding to the end of a Noweb link, and if
     there is some other text immediately following a Noweb link, we'll end up
     introducing a newline in the middle of the line, which is almost never
     desirable.

  4) Within each group, only mark the links at the end of the line (because
     there may be more than one link in a line) as needing a newline. By marking
     links as needing (or not needing) a newline, we can easily inject padding
     at the right spots in the next step.

     We can't add padding for the very last line in the group though, because
     that would be superfluous (we want to add newlines *between* consecutive
     blocks, not after every consecutive block).

  5) Using the information from the previous step, iterate through all ~BToken~
     values and whenever we see a ~BTokenLinkTarget~, inject the padding (if the
     link was marked as needing a newline).

The markers are just simple boolean values (one for each Noweb link found in the
block), which denote whether or not the Noweb link needs padding.

Now let's look at the implementation in detail. Step 1 is done in
[[f:getBlockContents]].

#+name: f:getBlockContents
#+begin_src haskell
getBlockContents :: [BToken] -> Text
getBlockContents blockBody = T.concat $ map bTokenToText blockBody
  where
    bTokenToText :: BToken -> Text
    bTokenToText = \case
      BTokenString text -> text
      BTokenPadding -> ""
      BTokenLinkTarget (LinkTargetOid _) ->
        <<link-marker>>
      BTokenLinkTarget {} -> ""
#+end_src

The ~<<link-marker>>~ is just a special marker we use to keep track of where the
links are inside the concatenated (single) ~Text~ value representing all of the
block contents.

#+name: link-marker
#+begin_src haskell
"\NUL_link"
#+end_src

Steps 2, 3, and 4 are all done inside [[f:getLinkPaddingBools]]. This function
returns a list of booleans that correspond to each of the Noweb links found. If
~True~, this means the link needs a trailing newline (padding) added to it.

#+name: f:getLinkPaddingBools
#+begin_src haskell
getLinkPaddingBools :: [BToken] -> [Bool]
getLinkPaddingBools blockBody =
  concatMap (concatMap padLastLinkOnThisLine)
    . map padLinesExceptLast -- (ref:f:getLinkPaddingBools-pad-lines-except-last)
    . groupBy consecutiveNowebLines
    . T.lines
    $ getBlockContents blockBody
  where
    consecutiveNowebLines :: Text -> Text -> Bool
    consecutiveNowebLines a b =
      (nowebLinkAtEndOfLine a)
        && (nowebLinkAtEndOfLine b) -- (ref:f:getLinkPaddingBools-nowebLinkAtEndOfLine)
        && (countLinks a > 0)
        && (countLinks b > 0)
    padLinesExceptLast :: [Text] -> [(Bool, Text)]
    padLinesExceptLast lines =
      reverse
        . (zip $ False : repeat True)
        $ reverse lines
    padLastLinkOnThisLine :: (Bool, Text) -> [Bool]
    padLastLinkOnThisLine (padLinksInThisLine, line)
      | padLinksInThisLine = case countLinks line of
          1 -> [True]
          n -> reverse $ True : replicate (n - 1) False -- (ref:f:getLinkPaddingBools-padLastLinkOnThisLine)
      | otherwise = replicate (countLinks line) False
    countLinks = T.count <<link-marker>>
#+end_src

We only pad the lines except for the last one
[[(f:getLinkPaddingBools-pad-lines-except-last)]], and ~padLastLinkOnThisLine~
[[(f:getLinkPaddingBools-padLastLinkOnThisLine)]] only pads the very last link found
in the line. The call to [[f:nowebLinkAtEndOfLine]]
[[(f:getLinkPaddingBools-nowebLinkAtEndOfLine)]] limits this padding to Noweb links
that are at the end of the line; Noweb links that are followed by non-newline
text is called an "embedded" link. See
[[id:c78d3f21-b295-4f19-b720-ab50dedb3252::ti:0102-pad-between-consecutive-links-except-embedded][ti:0102-pad-between-consecutive-links-except-embedded]] for an example of the
expected behavior here.

#+name: f:nowebLinkAtEndOfLine
#+begin_src haskell
nowebLinkAtEndOfLine :: Text -> Bool
nowebLinkAtEndOfLine line = <<link-marker>> `T.isSuffixOf` line
#+end_src

For the list of booleans we get from [[f:getLinkPaddingBools]], all we have to do is
go through all of the links in the block, adding a ~BTokenPaddding~ every time we
see a ~True~ boolean (~padLink~).

#+name: f:padConsecutiveNowebLinks
#+begin_src haskell
padConsecutiveNowebLinks :: [BToken] -> [BToken]
padConsecutiveNowebLinks blockBody =
  reverse
    . fst
    $ foldl' f ([], getLinkPaddingBools blockBody) blockBody
  where
    f (acc, bools) text@(BTokenString _) = (text : acc, bools)
    f (acc, bools) BTokenPadding = (acc, bools)
    f (acc, bools) link@(BTokenLinkTarget (LinkTargetLineLabel _)) =
      (link : acc, bools)
    f (acc, bools) linkTarget@(BTokenLinkTarget _) =
      case bools of
        (padLink : rest)
          | padLink -> (BTokenPadding : linkTarget : acc, rest)
          | otherwise -> (linkTarget : acc, rest)
        [] -> (linkTarget : acc, [])
#+end_src

See the test case inputs below for examples of this padding behavior.

  - [[id:c78d3f21-b295-4f19-b720-ab50dedb3252::ti:0102-pad-between-consecutive-links][ti:0102-pad-between-consecutive-links]]

  - [[id:c78d3f21-b295-4f19-b720-ab50dedb3252::ti:0102-pad-between-consecutive-links-except-embedded][ti:0102-pad-between-consecutive-links-except-embedded]]

** Additional tests

A ~#+begin_src~ without an accompanying language should trigger an error.

#+name: ti:0007-block-src-without-lang
#+header: :tangle test/Lilac/ParseSpec/0007-block-src-without-lang
#+begin_src org
,#+begin_src
,#+end_src
#+end_src

#+name: t:0007-block-src-without-lang
#+begin_src haskell
shouldFailWithMessage
  "0007-block-src-without-lang"
  "block type src requires a language specifier"
  11
#+end_src

Below we test the edge cases around detecting the end of a block.

#+name: ti:0007-blocks-ending
#+header: :tangle test/Lilac/ParseSpec/0007-blocks-ending
#+begin_src org
,#+name: empty
,#+begin_src sh
,#+end_src

,#+name: end not in beginning of line
,#+begin_src sh
 ,#+end_src
,#+end_src

,#+name: end has no preceding blank line
,#+begin_src sh
foo
,#+end_src

,#+name: end has preceding blank line
,#+begin_src sh
foo

,#+end_src

,#+name: end has preceding newline (multiple)
,#+begin_src sh
foo


,#+end_src

,#+name: ignore #+end_example because this is a src block
,#+begin_src sh
,#+end_example
,#+end_src
#+end_src

#+name: t:0007-blocks-ending
#+begin_src haskell
shouldParseWithState
  "0007-blocks-ending"
  ( nilOrgDoc
      <<Expected blocks for 0007-blocks-ending>>
      []
  )
  defaultOrgParserState
    { cellNames =
        Set.fromList
          [ "empty",
            "end has no preceding blank line",
            "end has preceding blank line",
            "end has preceding newline (multiple)",
            "end not in beginning of line",
            "ignore #+end_example because this is a src block"
          ]
    }
#+end_src

#+name: Expected blocks for 0007-blocks-ending
#+begin_src haskell
[ CBlock
    Block
      { parentOid = nilOid,
        meta =
          fromList
            [ ("__block_type", "src"),
              ("__src_language", "sh"),
              ("name", "empty")
            ],
        body = []
      },
  CBlock
    Block
      { parentOid = nilOid,
        meta =
          fromList
            [ ("__block_type", "src"),
              ("__src_language", "sh"),
              ("name", "end not in beginning of line")
            ],
        body = [BTokenString " #+end_src"]
      },
  CBlock
    Block
      { parentOid = nilOid,
        meta =
          fromList
            [ ("__block_type", "src"),
              ("__src_language", "sh"),
              ("name", "end has no preceding blank line")
            ],
        body = [BTokenString "foo"]
      },
  CBlock
    Block
      { parentOid = nilOid,
        meta =
          fromList
            [ ("__block_type", "src"),
              ("__src_language", "sh"),
              ("name", "end has preceding blank line")
            ],
        body = [BTokenString "foo\n"]
      },
  CBlock
    Block
      { parentOid = nilOid,
        meta =
          fromList
            [ ("__block_type", "src"),
              ("__src_language", "sh"),
              ("name", "end has preceding newline (multiple)")
            ],
        body = [BTokenString "foo\n\n"]
      },
  CBlock
    Block
      { parentOid = nilOid,
        meta =
          fromList
            [ ("__block_type", "src"),
              ("__src_language", "sh"),
              ("name", "ignore #+end_example because this is a src block")
            ],
        body = [BTokenString "#+end_example"]
      }
]
#+end_src

* List items
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       58f69993-599d-4389-ba47-3056b1bb2350
:END:

Lists are similar to [[id:16a68a74-d4cc-41a4-ac77-cbca77de2a31][headings]], because they give you a way to structure your
paragraphs.

Lists are made up of list items. A /list item/ is made up of a /list head/ and /list
body/. List heads encode the structure of cells that sit in the list bodies. So
list items encode both structure (with list heads) and content (with list
bodies). However unlike headings, a list item can have multiple other cells
(typically paragraphs) in its body, whereas a heading can only have a single
line of prose. Consider the following list:

#+begin_src org
  1) a

     b

     - c

     d

  2) e
#+end_src

Here the ~1)~ and ~2)~ are list heads for an /ordered/ list. The single dash ~-~ before
"c" is also a list head, but for an /unordered/ list. The list body for ~1)~
includes the paragraphs ~a~, ~b~, the nested list item ~c~, /and the paragraph/ ~d~. This
is another difference versus headings. That is, a list item can have content for
it /after/ backing out of a nested list item. To reiterate, in the list shown above
the ~d~ paragraph is part of the ordered list item ~1)~ even though the nested item
~c~ is just above it; this sort of structure is now allowed for headings. For
example, in the following situation

#+begin_src org
,* a
,** b
#+end_src

anything that comes after ~** b~ will be part of the ~** b~ heading; there is no way
to make it belong to ~* a~ (any such content must be placed /before/ ~** b~). List
items are not restricted like this, because they always have meaningful
indentation (a list head must be preceded by indentation), and this meaning of
each indentation level is tracked during the parse.

List bodies may have cells of the following type:

  - [[id:272567ab-4077-4a47-ae78-dfe77243ccc0][Paragraphs]]

    Example paragraph.

  - [[id:d0f60631-8b30-4ae1-992d-49c7b0ea38fb][Tables]]

    | Example | Table |
    |---------+-------|
    |       1 |     2 |

  - [[id:d1064535-5c98-4c80-a901-5cdc1fd5297f][Standalone math fragments]]

    \begin{equation}
    2 + 2 = 4
    \end{equation}

  - and of course, more list items (nesting)

      - Example nested item.

Vanilla Org allows more cell types like code blocks inside list bodies, but
Lilac does not. List items should be simple, and things like code blocks just
makes list items more complicated. The fact that we support tables and
standalone math fragments in list bodies is an accident, not a product of
design. Indeed, for typical use cases, you shouldn't need anything more than
paragraphs and other list items inside a list.

Below we have a look at some more detailed rules around how lists may be written
in an Org file. They are somewhat numerous because of all the edge cases we have
to account for.

** Ordered list items
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       32d34523-2148-4f2f-b2f3-2a2543a7d57c
:END:

Below are some detailed rules around /ordered/ list items, themselves expressed as
ordered list items with links to test cases as examples. The discussions around
space characters and indentation only make sense when viewed in the raw Org
file, not the woven HTML format.

  1) List items start out with 2-space indentation. This makes them easier to
     read from the raw Org file. The amount of additional indentation (in order
     to have nested list items) depends on where the text began in the parent
     item.

       - [[ti:0009-list-indentation-no-indent]]

       - [[ti:0009-list-indentation-proper-indent]]

  2) List items must be separated from each other with an empty blank line. This
     makes them easier to read when reading the Org file in plain text.

       - [[ti:0009-list-items-need-blank-line-separator]]

  3) Additional hard-wrapped lines for a list item must be aligned with the text
     of the first line.

       - [[ti:0005-paragraph-indentation-mismatch-over-list-body]]

       - [[ti:0005-paragraph-indentation-mismatch-over]]

       - [[ti:0005-paragraph-indentation-mismatch-under-list-body]]

       - [[ti:0005-paragraph-indentation-mismatch-initial-indent-invalid]]

       - [[ti:0005-paragraph-indentation-mismatch-initial-indent-invalid-between-levels]]

  4) List items can have more than one paragraph associated with them.

     This is an example of another paragraph which is still part of same list
     item, and has 5 spaces of indentation.

       - [[ti:0009-list-content-simple]]

  5) List items can be nested.

       1) This is a nested item, underneath "5)". It has 7 spaces of initial
          indentation (5 needed to line up with the column where the "L" is in
          "5) List" in the parent item, plus another 2 to indent one more level
          beyond that). Subsequent lines of this paragraph have 10 spaces of
          indentation to line up with the text above it.

     See:

       - [[ti:0009-list-indentation-nested]]

       - [[ti:0009-list-indentation-nested-with-text]]

       - [[ti:0009-paragraph-can-close-deeper-levels]]

  6) Unlike Markdown, the numbers of the list item must increase by one if there
     are additional items at the same level. And the first number of a level
     must start from 1.

       - [[ti:0009-ordered-lists-require-increment]]

       - [[ti:0009-ordered-lists-start-from-1]]

  7) The format for ordered list items requires a closing parenthesis
     immediately after the number. So ~1)~ is valid, but ~1.~ is invalid.

       - [[ti:0009-ordered-list-head-needs-parenthesis]]

  8) Only numbers may be used to denote item position. You cannot use letters or
     Roman numerals like ~a)~ or ~i)~.

       - [[ti:0009-ordered-list-head-cannot-use-letters]]

  9) There is no limit to the number of consecutive list items at the same
     level (technically, we use an ~Int~ type to encode the number, so it will
     eventually max out, but you'd need billions of items to hit this limit).

  10) The indentation of text changes slightly depending on the number of the
      item. For example, this line has 6 spaces of indentation, unlike the
      paragraphs in item "4)" above, which uses 5 spaces.

        1) Similarly, this child item has text which has 11 spaces of
           indentation, unlike the 10 spaces of indentation in the ordered child
           item under item "5)" above.

      See:

        - [[ti:0009-lists-require-precise-indentation]]

  11) There can only be up to 2 additional levels of nesting (for a total of 3
      levels of items). This is because excessive nesting is an anti-pattern.
      See the ~lilac_maxListLevel~ [[id:84edd33a-a146-4a46-ac0a-cd1c3d0926d3][Lilac extension]] for customizing this behavior.

        - [[ti:0009-list-nesting-max]]

        - [[ti:0009-list-nesting-max-8]]

        - [[ti:0009-lists-prohibited]]

  12) Ordered list items may have unordered list items as children, and vice
      versa. However you may not mix the two types of items at the same level.

        - [[ti:0009-paragraph-can-separate-mixed-lists]]

        - [[ti:0009-lists-cannot-be-mixed-on-their-own]]

        - [[ti:0009-lists-cannot-be-mixed-on-their-own-ordered]]

  13) A list item may include a table. However the table cannot be on the same
      line as the list item head. Below is an example of such a table:

      | foo | bar |
      |-----+-----|
      | x   | y   |

        - [[ti:0012-tables-inside-lists]]

  14) Similarly, a list item may include mathematical equations. However, such
      an equation must be on a separate line from the list item head, just like
      for tables (see Rule 13 above).

      \[
         a + b = c
      \]

      The equation above (and this paragraph) are still part of Rule 14.

        - [[ti:0010-standalone-math-inside-lists]]

  15) Lists may be empty. For example, the following child list is valid even
      though it does not have any content:

        1)

        2)

        3)

      See:

        - [[ti:0009-list-indentation-nested]]

** Unordered list items

Unordered list items use a single dash character ~-~, and always use 2-space
indentation (if you are only using unordered list items for nesting). Below is
an example:

#+begin_src org
  - one

  - two

      - foo

      - bar

  - three
#+end_src

Here's an example of mixing ordered and unordered lists:

#+begin_src org
  - foo

  - bar

      1) one

      2) two

           - baz

           - quux

         Note how this paragraph is under item "2", because it is indented the
         same way as "two", not "quux", above.

      3) three

  - dog
#+end_src

You may mix ordered and unordered lists at the same indentation level, as long
as they are not consecutive (you need some intervening cells, such as the "quux"
paragraph below):

#+begin_src org
  - foo

      1) one

      2) two

    quux

      - a

      - b
#+end_src

** Indentation and ~ListMarker~

Parsing list items is tricky because of meaningful indentation (whitespace).
Keeping track of the current level of indentation requires additional state
during parsing, which we encode with ~Level~ (used by ~listLevels~
[[(data:OrgParserState-listLevels)]] in [[data:OrgParserState]]).

#+name: data:Level
#+begin_src haskell
type Level :: Type
data Level = Level
  { indent :: Int,
    number :: Maybe Int
  }
  deriving stock (Eq, Show)
#+end_src

This keeps track of the indentations and the last seen number (only for ordered
lists) of each (nested) level of indentation thus far during parsing. Whenever
we see a list head like ~1)~ or ~-~, we record the number used as well as the
indentations we expect for the list head and list body. The main point of
~listLevels~ is to ensure that the input is correctly formatted, as well as to
detect whenever we start nesting an additional level, or if we back out (finish)
one or more levels of nesting (when we see dedented text).

As we observe how these ~listLevels~ change during parsing, we create "start" and
"end" markers for list items at a particular level, which are the values of the
[[data:ListMarker]] type. The ~ListMarker~ objects are parsed as their own cell as a
~CListItem~ (see [[data:Cell]]).

#+name: data:ListMarker
#+begin_src haskell
type ListMarker :: Type
data ListMarker
  = LOrderedStart
  | LOrderedEnd
  | LUnorderedStart
  | LUnorderedEnd
  | LItemStart
  | LItemEnd
  deriving stock (Eq, Ord, Generic, Show)
  deriving anyclass (FromJSON, ToJSON)
#+end_src

We take this model from HTML, which uses ~<ol>~ and ~</ol>~ to encode the start and
end of ordered lists (~<ul>~ and ~</ul>~ for unordered lists), and ~<li>~ and ~</li>~ to
mark the start and end of a single list item.

#+name: tbl:List markers
#+caption: ~ListMarker~ values and their HTML equivalents.
| ListMarker      | HTML equivalent |
|-----------------+-----------------|
| ~LOrderedStart~   | ~<ol>~            |
| ~LOrderedEnd~     | ~</ol>~           |
| ~LUnorderedStart~ | ~<ul>~            |
| ~LUnorderedEnd~   | ~</ul>~           |
| ~LItemStart~      | ~<li>~            |
| ~LItemEnd~        | ~</li>~           |

In summary when we parse a list item, we parse it as a combination of ~CListItem~
and other cell types for the list structure and content, respectively. This lets
us parse things into a single global list of cells in [[f:cellParser]], without a
need to nest them inside a recursive data structure (such as in
https://markkarpov.com/tutorial/megaparsec.html#nested-indented-list).

** Parsing initial indentation to close list items
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       5990dd7f-2f37-4a9e-96ea-6cc6b2e20dc0
:END:

Before getting into parsing the list items and taking into account all of the
[[id:32d34523-2148-4f2f-b2f3-2a2543a7d57c][rules]], let's first consider case where we just want to /close off/ the
list items, as this is simpler than creating list items. We want to do this
every time we parse initial indentation where there is no list head. For
example, in the following

#+begin_src org
  1) a

       - b

     c
#+end_src

the indentation for paragraph ~c~ should by itself close off the preceding
unordered list item (~- b~). We can close off existing list items depending on the
amount of indentation.

  1) If there is no indentation, we close off /all/ list items.

  2) If there is maximum (already-seen) indentation (to line up with the
     most-nested list item), we don't close off any list items because we're
     just continuing the most-nested list item.

  3) Otherwise, we have some amount of indentation and we only want to close off
     some list levels, depending on how much indentation we detect.

If there is more indentation than the maximum, then we are nesting another level
and don't need to close off anything. That's why this situation is not listed
above.

Point 1 is captured by ~zeroIndent~ in [[f:listItemsEndParser]]. Points 2 and 3 are
captured by ~someIndent~ in the same function.

#+name: f:zeroIndent
#+begin_src haskell
zeroIndent :: OrgParser (Int, Int)
zeroIndent = do
  pState <- get
  _ <- MP.eof <|> MP.notFollowedBy (char ' ')
  let listLevelsToClose = length pState.listLevels
      continuationIndent = 0 :: Int
  pure (listLevelsToClose, continuationIndent)
#+end_src

In [[f:zeroIndent]] we return the number of list levels to close off (in this case
all levels that we've detected so far), and the continuation indent. The
continuation indent is used by other parsers such as the [[f:paragraphParser]] to
see how to join up multiple lines into the same cell, assuming those lines have
the same continuation indent (the lines /continue/ if it's the same indent, hence
the name). For the case of [[f:zeroIndent]], we set the continuation indent to 0
because there is no indentation, and any subsequent lines belonging to the same
cell should /not/ have any indentation (it is considered invalid input if we see
surprise indentation; see [[t:0005-paragraph-indentation-mismatch-over]]).

For [[f:someIndent]], we just try to line up the amount of indentation found with
the existing list levels we have and how much indentation they expect.

#+name: f:someIndent
#+begin_src haskell
someIndent :: OrgParser (Int, Int)
someIndent = do
  pState <- get
  indentFound <- T.length <$> onlySpaces
  let levelsToDropPerIndentationIncrement :: [(Int, Int)]
      levelsToDropPerIndentationIncrement =
        zip [0 ..] -- (ref:f:someIndent-zip0)
          . map getContinuationIndentForLevel
          $ pState.listLevels -- (ref:f:someIndent-pState.listLevels)
      matchingIndent =
        find
          (\(_, continuationIndent) -> indentFound == continuationIndent)
          levelsToDropPerIndentationIncrement
  case matchingIndent of
    Nothing -> fail "indentation does not fit into existing levels"
    Just x@(_levelsToDrop, _continuationIndent) -> pure x
#+end_src

The ~pState.listLevels~ [[(f:someIndent-pState.listLevels)]] is grown by prepending to
the list in [[f:listItemNester]]. So the most-deeply-nested level will be at the
head of the list in ~pState.listLevels~. That's why we use ~zip [0 ..]~
[[(f:someIndent-zip0)]] --- we want to drop 0 levels if we see indentation matching
the most deeply-nested list item.

The [[f:getContinuationIndentForLevel]] computes the total indentation for a list
level. For example, in

#+begin_src org
  1) foo
#+end_src

we compute the initial 2 spaces before the ~1)~, the ~1)~ itself (two characters),
plus a space character, or a total continuation indent of 5. The dynamic portion
here is the number, which will grow as the list grows (e.g., ~10)~ will have a
total continuation indent of 6), which is why we use a function here instead of
a constant.

#+name: f:getContinuationIndentForLevel
#+begin_src haskell
getContinuationIndentForLevel :: Level -> Int
getContinuationIndentForLevel level =
  level.indent + maybeNum + punctuation + space
  where
    punctuation :: Int
    punctuation = 1
    space :: Int
    space = 1
    maybeNum :: Int
    maybeNum = case level.number of
      Just n -> length $ (show n :: String)
      Nothing -> 0
#+end_src

Once we determine the amount of levels we need to close off (by parsing
[[f:zeroIndent]] or [[f:someIndent]]), we just go through the list markers we've already
seen in ~listMarkers~ and close them off via [[f:getEndingListMarkers]]. This is what
we do in [[f:listItemsEndParser]].

#+name: f:listItemsEndParser
#+begin_src haskell
listItemsEndParser :: OrgParser Cell
listItemsEndParser = do
  pState <- get
  when (null pState.listMarkers) $ do
    fail "no pending ListMarker objects to close off" -- (ref:list-items-end-failure-with-no-input)
  when (pState.continuationIndent /= 0) $ do
    fail "not at beginning of line"
  (levelsToDrop, continuationIndent) <- zeroIndent <|> someIndent
  let markers = getEndingListMarkers levelsToDrop pState.listLevels
      markersToDrop = levelsToDrop * 2 -- (ref:f:listItemsEndParser-markersToDrop)
  put
    pState
      { listLevels = drop levelsToDrop pState.listLevels,
        listMarkers = drop markersToDrop pState.listMarkers,
        continuationIndent = continuationIndent
      }
  pure $ CListItem markers
#+end_src

In [[f:listItemsEndParser]], we initially fail the parser if there are no pending
~ListMarker~ objects (no list levels to close off), or if we're not at the
beginning of the line (because otherwise we cannot measure indentation
accurately), by checking ~pState.continuationIndent~. This check of
~pState.continuationIndent~ is required because there is no easy way to check for
the just-parsed token (or character) in Megaparsec --- all parsers only see
what's in front of it. So then it's up to other parsers to set this to some
value greater than ~1~ (we do this in [[f:listHeadIndentLevelParser]] which is part of
[[f:listItemStartParser]])

The ~markersToDrop~ [[(f:listItemsEndParser-markersToDrop)]] is always twice the
number of ~levelsToDrop~ because we always add 2 (~LItemStart~ plus either
~LOrderedStart~ or ~LUnorderedStart~) for each level (see [[f:listItemNester]]).

If we detect the maximum allowed indentation, then there are no levels to drop,
and hence, we'll end up returning ~CListItem []~ which doesn't hold any meaning.
Avoiding this is a bit difficult; if we just fail the parser outright, then the
amount of parsed indentation will have to be parsed by another downstream parser
(this can get messy). So instead we just return ~CListItem []~ and then filter
these out in [[f:orgDocParser]]. This way we decide once and for all how to deal
with leading indentation (and how this closes off existing list items) in
[[f:listItemsEndParser]].

The [[f:getEndingListMarkers]] function understands how to close off list levels
based on whether the level uses an ordered or unordered list item.

#+name: f:getEndingListMarkers
#+begin_src haskell
getEndingListMarkers :: Int -> [Level] -> [ListMarker]
getEndingListMarkers levelsToDrop = concatMap f . take levelsToDrop
  where
    f :: Level -> [ListMarker]
    f level = case level.number of
      Nothing -> [LItemEnd, LUnorderedEnd]
      Just _ -> [LItemEnd, LOrderedEnd]
#+end_src

** Parsing list heads

In the [[id:5990dd7f-2f37-4a9e-96ea-6cc6b2e20dc0][previous section]] we considered how to close off list items based purely
on indentation. Parsing list heads is similar (because list heads can also close
off list items), but more complicated because we also have to take into account
how to create list items (list levels). This means we have to deal with all of
the possible values of [[data:ListMarker]] here.

Parsing the list head (and therefore the [[data:Level]] of a list item) is
straightforward. For unordered lists we just want to see a dash, returning
~Nothing~ [[(f:unorderedHeadParser-no-number)]] because there is no list number.

#+name: f:unorderedHeadParser
#+begin_src haskell
unorderedHeadParser :: OrgParser (Maybe Int)
unorderedHeadParser = do
  _ <- char '-'
  pure Nothing -- (ref:f:unorderedHeadParser-no-number)
#+end_src

For ordered lists we want to see the number and closing parenthesis.

#+name: f:orderedHeadParser
#+begin_src haskell
orderedHeadParser :: OrgParser (Maybe Int)
orderedHeadParser = do
  num <- MP.takeWhile1P (Just "ordered list number") isDigit
  _ <- char ')'
  let n = readMaybe (T.unpack num) :: Maybe Int
  when (isNothing n) $ do
    fail $ "could not parse " <> show (T.unpack num) <> " as Int"
  pure n
#+end_src

We check if the number looks like an ~Int~ with ~readMaybe~.

For both cases we also parse leading indentation and also an additional space at
the end to separate the list head from the body. This is what
[[f:listHeadIndentLevelParser]] does.

#+name: f:listHeadIndentLevelParser
#+begin_src haskell
listHeadIndentLevelParser :: OrgParser Level
listHeadIndentLevelParser = do
  minimalIndentation -- (ref:f:listHeadIndentLevelParser-minimalIndentation)
  spaces <- onlySpaces
  listHead <- unorderedHeadParser <|> orderedHeadParser
  let level =
        Level
          { indent = T.length spaces,
            number = listHead
          }
  afterListHead level
  pure level
  where
    minimalIndentation :: OrgParser ()
    minimalIndentation =
      void
        . MP.try
        . MP.lookAhead
        . MP.label "minimal indentation"
        $ MP.count 2 (char ' ')
    afterListHead :: Level -> OrgParser () -- (ref:f:listHeadIndentLevelParser-afterListHead)
    afterListHead level =
      void
        $ MP.choice
          [ MP.eof,
            void $ string "\n",
            void $ seeingContentAfterListHead level
          ]
    seeingContentAfterListHead :: Level -> OrgParser ()
    seeingContentAfterListHead level = do
      _ <- char ' '
      MP.notFollowedBy (char ' ')
      modify
        (\s -> s {continuationIndent = getContinuationIndentForLevel level})
#+end_src

The ~minimalIndentation~ [[(f:listHeadIndentLevelParser-minimalIndentation)]] expects
there to be at least 2 spaces preceding a list item. This follows Rule 1 from
[[id:32d34523-2148-4f2f-b2f3-2a2543a7d57c][Ordered list items]].

The ~afterListHead~ parser [[(f:listHeadIndentLevelParser-afterListHead)]]
preemptively checks what's in the input /after/ parsing the list head. In the case
where it sees some other non-empty content, it expects some other parser to
continue parsing. So inside ~seeingContentAfterListHead~ we set the
~continuationIndent~ attribute to indentation of the current level, because we
expect another parser to help us finish parsing the line. Then at the
[[f:cellParser]] level, we set this back to ~0~ for any parser that is expected to
consume content and end with a newline. This lets [[f:listItemsEndParser]] know
whether it is looking at EOF or at the beginning of a line when it inspects
~continuationIndent~. If Megaparsec can let us easily check the previous character
that was parsed, this scheme of using a stateful ~continuationIndent~ field
wouldn't be necessary.

Note, however, that [[f:listHeadIndentLevelParser]] only checks for textual
correctness. It doesn't check whether what's parsed is semantically correct. For
example, we wouldn't want to blindly parse the number of spaces in the indent
(instead we would want to check which indents are expected, given any previous
list items). Similarly we would want to check whether the list head number (for
ordered lists) is the expected (incremented) number from the previously-seen
list head number, if any (or if there is no previous number, that this number is
1).

We have to perform these additional checks by comparing the output of
[[f:listHeadIndentLevelParser]] against the existing [[data:OrgParserState]]
(~listLevels~). This is what we do in [[f:listItemStartParser]] below, which wraps
around [[f:listHeadIndentLevelParser]]
[[(f:listItemStartParser-listHeadIndentLevelParser)]].

#+name: f:listItemStartParser
#+begin_src haskell
listItemStartParser :: OrgParser Cell
listItemStartParser = do
  _ <- MP.try $ MP.lookAhead listHeadIndentLevelParser -- (ref:f:listItemStartParser-lookAhead)
  pState <- get
  let levels = pState.listLevels
  level <- listHeadIndentLevelParser -- (ref:f:listItemStartParser-listHeadIndentLevelParser)
  listMarkers <-
    if nestsBeyond levels level
      then listItemNester level levels
      else listItemFitter level levels
  pure $ CListItem listMarkers
  where
    nestsBeyond :: [Level] -> Level -> Bool -- (ref:f:listItemStartParser-nestsBeyond)
    nestsBeyond [] _ = True
    nestsBeyond (deepestLevel : _) level =
      getContinuationIndentForLevel deepestLevel + 2 == level.indent
#+end_src

The use of ~MP.lookAhead~ [[(f:listItemStartParser-lookAhead)]] is needed because
otherwise as soon as Megaparsec sees any indentation (as part of invoking
[[f:listHeadIndentLevelParser]]) it will expect to see a list head; obviously this
is not always the case as not all indentation leads to a list head. This is why
we have to perform this look-ahead preemptively.

The ~nestsBeyond~ function [[(f:listItemStartParser-nestsBeyond)]] just checks whether
the indentation we found in the (just-parsed) level is the same as the
continuation indent of the most-deeply-nested level we have so far, plus 2 extra
spaces. This is how we nest one level deeper than the current level. If this is
the case, we delegate to [[f:listItemNester]]. Otherwise the list item is merely
continuing some existing list, for which we delegate to [[f:listItemFitter]].

The use of 2 extra spaces is necessary because a list item must always be
indented 2 spaces relative to the just-preceding paragraph.

** Nesting the list item beyond the current level

[[f:listItemNester]] prepends starting markers to ~listMarkers~. We use this
information in other places when we want to know how to un-nest an item (close
it off), such as in [[f:listItemsEndParser]] where we add corresponding closing
markers with [[f:getEndingListMarkers]]. The other place we use ~listMarkers~ is in
[[f:listItemFitter]].

#+name: f:listItemNester
#+begin_src haskell
listItemNester :: Level -> [Level] -> OrgParser [ListMarker]
listItemNester level levels = do
  case level.number of
    Just 1 -> pure ()
    Just n -> fail $ "starting list head number must be 1; got " <> show n
    Nothing -> pure ()
  pState <- get
  let newMarker = case level.number of
        Just _ -> LOrderedStart
        Nothing -> LUnorderedStart
      opc = pState.orgParserConf
  when (opc.maxListLevel > 0 && length levels == opc.maxListLevel) $ do
    fail
      $ "max nesting depth exceeded: cannot have more than "
      <> show opc.maxListLevel
      <> " levels of list items"
  put
    pState -- (ref:f:listItemNester-update-pState)
      { listLevels = level : levels,
        listMarkers = LItemStart : newMarker : pState.listMarkers
      }
  pure [newMarker, LItemStart]
#+end_src

We enforce some additional rules here, such as forcing the nesting list head to
start off with 1 (see [[t:0009-ordered-lists-start-from-1]]) if it is an ordered
list. The rest of the function just updates the ~pState~ to have the additional
~level~ and also grows the ~listMarkers~ accordingly
[[(f:listItemNester-update-pState)]].

** Fitting the list item into an existing level

The goal of [[f:listItemFitter]] is twofold:

  1) continue an existing list, and

  2) close off (finish) pending levels which are still open.

The first task is easy --- we just append ~[LItemEnd, LItemStart]~ to signal that
we are closing off the existing list item (at the same level) to start a new
list item (also at the same level).

The second task is a bit more involved. We have to "close" any existing levels
that are more deeply nested, just like in [[f:listItemsEndParser]]. For example, in
the following list

#+begin_src org
  1) foo

     - bar

       1) baz

  2) quux
#+end_src

when we hit ~quux~ we have to close off the list environments for ~baz~, then ~bar~.
However, unlike [[f:listItemsEndParser]], we have to /continue/ the existing list
level that we fit into. For ordered list items, we have to drop an extra level
in ~listLevels~ in the ~fitter~ function [[(f:listItemFitter-extra-level-drop)]], and
then replace it with the (incremented) level to continue it.

#+name: f:listItemFitter
#+begin_src haskell
listItemFitter :: Level -> [Level] -> OrgParser [ListMarker]
listItemFitter level levels =
  case find (\(_, l) -> l.indent == level.indent) (zip [0 ..] levels) of
    Nothing -> fail "list item: invalid indentation"
    Just (levelsToDrop, lvl)
      | isJust level.number && isJust lvl.number ->
          let expectedNum = fmap (+ 1) lvl.number
           in if level.number == expectedNum
                then fitter levelsToDrop
                else
                  fail
                    $ "expected incremented list head "
                    <> (show . fromJust $ expectedNum)
                    <> " but got "
                    <> (show . fromJust $ level.number)
      | isNothing level.number && isNothing lvl.number -> fitter levelsToDrop
      | isJust lvl.number -> fail "expected ORDERED list item at this level"
      | otherwise -> fail "expected UNORDERED list item at this level"
  where
    fitter :: Int -> OrgParser [ListMarker]
    fitter levelsToDrop = do
      pState <- get
      let markers = getEndingListMarkers levelsToDrop pState.listLevels
      put
        pState
          { listLevels =
              level : drop (levelsToDrop + 1) pState.listLevels, -- (ref:f:listItemFitter-extra-level-drop)
            listMarkers = drop (levelsToDrop * 2) pState.listMarkers
          }
      pure $ markers <> [LItemEnd, LItemStart]
#+end_src

We use [[f:listItemStartParser]] and [[f:listItemsEndParser]] inside [[f:cellParser]]; the
former identifies the beginnings of list items, while the latter recognizes
their (complete) end when there is no more indentation.

** Tests

There are [[id:32d34523-2148-4f2f-b2f3-2a2543a7d57c][so many rules]] associated with ordered lists; we try to check a few of
them here.

#+name: ti:0009-list-indentation-no-indent
#+header: :tangle test/Lilac/ParseSpec/0009-list-indentation-no-indent
#+begin_src org
1)

2)
#+end_src

#+name: t:0009-list-indentation-no-indent
#+begin_src haskell
shouldParseWith
  "0009-list-indentation-no-indent"
  $ nilOrgDoc
    [ plainParagraph "1)",
      plainParagraph "2)"
    ]
    []
#+end_src

The list-like text is parsed as plain paragraphs in
[[t:0009-list-indentation-no-indent]], because the lack of indentation means we
don't recognize the ~1)~ and ~2)~ as list items.

Here's a list with the (proper) 2-space indent.

#+name: ti:0009-list-indentation-proper-indent
#+header: :tangle test/Lilac/ParseSpec/0009-list-indentation-proper-indent
#+begin_src org
  1)

  2)
#+end_src

#+name: t:0009-list-indentation-proper-indent
#+begin_src haskell
shouldParseWith
  "0009-list-indentation-proper-indent"
  $ nilOrgDoc
    [ CListItem
        [ LOrderedStart,
          LItemStart
        ],
      CListItem
        [ LItemEnd,
          LItemStart
        ],
      CListItem
        [ LItemEnd,
          LOrderedEnd
        ]
    ]
    []
#+end_src

Ordered lists require a closing parenthesis ~)~ after the number.

#+name: ti:0009-ordered-list-head-needs-parenthesis
#+header: :tangle test/Lilac/ParseSpec/0009-ordered-list-head-needs-parenthesis
#+begin_src org
  1.

  2.
#+end_src

#+name: t:0009-ordered-list-head-needs-parenthesis
#+begin_src haskell
shouldFailWithError
  "0009-ordered-list-head-needs-parenthesis"
  (utoks "." <> etoks ")" <> elabel "ordered list number")
  3
#+end_src

Ordered lists may only use numbers, not letters or Roman numerals.

#+name: ti:0009-ordered-list-head-cannot-use-letters
#+header: :tangle test/Lilac/ParseSpec/0009-ordered-list-head-cannot-use-letters
#+begin_src org
  a)
#+end_src

#+name: t:0009-ordered-list-head-cannot-use-letters
#+begin_src haskell
shouldFailWithMessage
  "0009-ordered-list-head-cannot-use-letters"
  "invalid indentation"
  2
#+end_src

Here's a nested example.

#+name: ti:0009-list-indentation-nested
#+header: :tangle test/Lilac/ParseSpec/0009-list-indentation-nested
#+begin_src org
  1)

       -

       -

  2)

       1)

            -

  3)
#+end_src

#+name: t:0009-list-indentation-nested
#+begin_src haskell
shouldParseWith
  "0009-list-indentation-nested"
  $ nilOrgDoc
    [ CListItem
        [ LOrderedStart, ---- Start outer ordered list.
          LItemStart -------- Start outer "1)".
        ],
      CListItem
        [ LUnorderedStart, -- Start inner bullets under "1)".
          LItemStart -------- Inner bullet start.
        ],
      CListItem
        [ LItemEnd, --------- Inner bullet end.
          LItemStart -------- Inner bullet start.
        ],
      CListItem
        [ LItemEnd, --------- Inner bullet end.
          LUnorderedEnd, ---- End of inner bullets under "1)".
          LItemEnd, --------- End of outer "1)".
          LItemStart -------- Start of outer "2)".
        ],
      CListItem
        [ LOrderedStart, ---- Start of nested ordered list.
          LItemStart -------- Start of nested "1)".
        ],
      CListItem
        [ LUnorderedStart, -- Start deeply nested bullets.
          LItemStart -------- Deeply nested bullet start.
        ],
      CListItem
        [ LItemEnd, --------- Deeply nested bullet end.
          LUnorderedEnd, ---- End of deeply nested bullets.
          LItemEnd, --------- End of nested "1)".
          LOrderedEnd, ------ End of nested ordered list.
          LItemEnd, --------- End of outer "2)".
          LItemStart -------- Start of outer "3)".
        ],
      CListItem
        [ LItemEnd, --------- End of outer "3)".
          LOrderedEnd ------- End outer ordered list.
        ]
    ]
    []
#+end_src

Nesting is limited to 2 additional levels, for a total of 3 levels by default.

#+name: ti:0009-list-nesting-max
#+header: :tangle test/Lilac/ParseSpec/0009-list-nesting-max
#+begin_src org
  - 1

      - 2

          - 3

              - 4
#+end_src

#+name: t:0009-list-nesting-max
#+begin_src haskell
shouldFailWithMessage
  "0009-list-nesting-max"
  "max nesting depth exceeded: cannot have more than 3 levels of list items"
  49
#+end_src

You can change the maximum nesting behavior with ~lilac_maxListLevel~ [[id:84edd33a-a146-4a46-ac0a-cd1c3d0926d3][Lilac
extension]]. In the test below we increase the total list level to 9.

#+name: ti:0009-list-nesting-max-8
#+header: :tangle test/Lilac/ParseSpec/0009-list-nesting-max-8
#+begin_src org
,#+lilac_maxListLevel: 8
  - 1

      - 2

          - 3

              - 4

                  - 5

                      - 6

                          - 7

                              - 8

                                  - 9
#+end_src

#+name: t:0009-list-nesting-max-8
#+begin_src haskell
shouldFailWithMessage
  "0009-list-nesting-max-8"
  "max nesting depth exceeded: cannot have more than 8 levels of list items"
  228
#+end_src

Notice how the parser failed only after parsing the last level which exceeded
the 8 levels allowed.

We can prohibit list items entirely, much like how we can prohibit them in
[[t:0004-headings-prohibited]].

#+name: ti:0009-lists-prohibited
#+header: :tangle test/Lilac/ParseSpec/0009-lists-prohibited
#+begin_src org
,#+lilac_maxListLevel: -1
  - 1
#+end_src

#+name: t:0009-lists-prohibited
#+begin_src haskell
shouldFailWithMessage
  "0009-list-prohibited"
  "lists prohibited because lilac_maxListLevel < 0"
  29
#+end_src

Now we test whether list items can also hold content (other cells) inside it, in
this case paragraph cells.

#+name: ti:0009-list-content-simple
#+header: :tangle test/Lilac/ParseSpec/0009-list-content-simple
#+begin_src org
  1) First
     paragraph.

     Second paragraph.

  2) Another paragraph.
#+end_src

#+name: t:0009-list-content-simple
#+begin_src haskell
shouldParseWith
  "0009-list-content-simple"
  $ nilOrgDoc
    [ CListItem
        [ LOrderedStart,
          LItemStart
        ],
      plainParagraph "First paragraph.",
      plainParagraph "Second paragraph.",
      CListItem
        [ LItemEnd,
          LItemStart
        ],
      plainParagraph "Another paragraph.",
      CListItem
        [ LItemEnd,
          LOrderedEnd
        ]
    ]
    []
#+end_src

Here's a more involved example using paragraph text for list items, following
the pattern from [[ti:0009-list-indentation-nested]]. Note how the parsed list
structure is still there, except now we have ~CParagraph~ cells interspersed in
between them.

#+name: ti:0009-list-indentation-nested-with-text
#+header: :tangle test/Lilac/ParseSpec/0009-list-indentation-nested-with-text
#+begin_src org
Foo.

  1) one

       - a

       - b

  2) two

       1) two-point-one

          hello

            - X

  3) three

Bar.
#+end_src

#+name: t:0009-list-indentation-nested-with-text
#+begin_src haskell
shouldParseWith
  "0009-list-indentation-nested-with-text"
  $ nilOrgDoc
    [ plainParagraph "Foo.",
      CListItem
        [ LOrderedStart, ---- Start outer ordered list.
          LItemStart -------- Start outer "1)".
        ],
      plainParagraph "one",
      CListItem
        [ LUnorderedStart, -- Start inner bullets under "1)".
          LItemStart -------- Inner bullet start.
        ],
      plainParagraph "a",
      CListItem
        [ LItemEnd, --------- Inner bullet end.
          LItemStart -------- Inner bullet start.
        ],
      plainParagraph "b",
      CListItem
        [ LItemEnd, --------- Inner bullet end.
          LUnorderedEnd, ---- End of inner bullets under "1)".
          LItemEnd, --------- End of outer "1)".
          LItemStart -------- Start of outer "2)".
        ],
      plainParagraph "two",
      CListItem
        [ LOrderedStart, ---- Start of nested ordered list.
          LItemStart -------- Start of nested "1)".
        ],
      plainParagraph "two-point-one",
      plainParagraph "hello",
      CListItem
        [ LUnorderedStart, -- Start deeply nested bullets.
          LItemStart -------- Deeply nested bullet start.
        ],
      plainParagraph "X",
      CListItem
        [ LItemEnd, --------- Deeply nested bullet end.
          LUnorderedEnd, ---- End of deeply nested bullets.
          LItemEnd, --------- End of nested "1)".
          LOrderedEnd, ------ End of nested ordered list.
          LItemEnd, --------- End of outer "2)".
          LItemStart -------- Start of outer "3)".
        ],
      plainParagraph "three",
      CListItem
        [ LItemEnd, --------- End of outer "3)".
          LOrderedEnd ------- End outer ordered list.
        ],
      plainParagraph "Bar."
    ]
    []
#+end_src

Check whether we can detect invalid indentations for paragraphs that are
indented (indented due to them being inside a list). The first case is when the
line is indented too much in the list body.

#+name: ti:0005-paragraph-indentation-mismatch-over-list-body
#+header: :tangle test/Lilac/ParseSpec/0005-paragraph-indentation-mismatch-over-list-body
#+begin_src org
  1) Foo
       bar
     baz.
#+end_src

#+name: t:0005-paragraph-indentation-mismatch-over-list-body
#+begin_src haskell
shouldFailWithError
  "0005-paragraph-indentation-mismatch-over-list-body"
  (utoks " ")
  14
#+end_src

The same rule about keeping the same indentation applies for plain paragraphs
outside of list environments.

#+name: ti:0005-paragraph-indentation-mismatch-over
#+header: :tangle test/Lilac/ParseSpec/0005-paragraph-indentation-mismatch-over
#+begin_src org
Foo
  bar
baz.
#+end_src

#+name: t:0005-paragraph-indentation-mismatch-over
#+begin_src haskell
shouldFailWithError
  "0005-paragraph-indentation-mismatch-over"
  (utoks " ")
  4
#+end_src

Under-indentation is also a problem for continuation lines inside a single
paragraph block.

#+name: ti:0005-paragraph-indentation-mismatch-under-list-body
#+header: :tangle test/Lilac/ParseSpec/0005-paragraph-indentation-mismatch-under-list-body
#+begin_src org
  1) Foo
   bar
     baz.
#+end_src

#+name: t:0005-paragraph-indentation-mismatch-under-list-body
#+begin_src haskell
shouldFailWithError
  "0005-paragraph-indentation-mismatch-under-list-body"
  (utoks "b" <> etoks " ")
  12
#+end_src

If we have some number of list levels already, then the paragraph must fit into
one of those levels (it cannot have some arbitrary level of indentation) if
there is non-zero indentation.

#+name: ti:0005-paragraph-indentation-mismatch-initial-indent-invalid
#+header: :tangle test/Lilac/ParseSpec/0005-paragraph-indentation-mismatch-initial-indent-invalid
#+begin_src org
  1) Foo
     bar.

 Invalid indentation (this paragraph).
#+end_src

#+name: t:0005-paragraph-indentation-mismatch-initial-indent-invalid
#+begin_src haskell
shouldFailWithMessage
  "0005-paragraph-indentation-mismatch-initial-indent-invalid"
  "indentation does not fit into existing levels"
  21
#+end_src

#+name: ti:0005-paragraph-indentation-mismatch-initial-indent-invalid-between-levels
#+header: :tangle test/Lilac/ParseSpec/0005-paragraph-indentation-mismatch-initial-indent-invalid-between-levels
#+begin_src org
  1) Foo
     bar.

       - Baz.

   Invalid indentation (this paragraph).
#+end_src

#+name: t:0005-paragraph-indentation-mismatch-initial-indent-invalid-between-levels
#+begin_src haskell
shouldFailWithMessage
  "0005-paragraph-indentation-mismatch-initial-indent-invalid-between-levels"
  "indentation does not fit into existing levels"
  38
#+end_src

Paragraphs should be able to close list environments, depending on their
indentation. For example, in the test below the paragraph ~Three~ should be able
to close off the unordered list items.

#+name: ti:0009-paragraph-can-close-deeper-levels
#+header: :tangle test/Lilac/ParseSpec/0009-paragraph-can-close-deeper-levels
#+begin_src org
  1) One

     Two
     x

       - Bar

           - Baz

     Three
     a b
     c

Four
#+end_src

#+name: t:0009-paragraph-can-close-deeper-levels
#+begin_src haskell
shouldParseWith
  "0009-paragraph-can-close-deeper-levels"
  $ nilOrgDoc
    [ CListItem
        [ LOrderedStart,
          LItemStart
        ],
      plainParagraph "One",
      plainParagraph "Two x",
      CListItem
        [ LUnorderedStart,
          LItemStart
        ],
      plainParagraph "Bar",
      CListItem
        [ LUnorderedStart,
          LItemStart
        ],
      plainParagraph "Baz",
      CListItem
        [ LItemEnd,
          LUnorderedEnd,
          LItemEnd,
          LUnorderedEnd
        ],
      plainParagraph "Three a b c",
      CListItem
        [ LItemEnd,
          LOrderedEnd
        ],
      plainParagraph "Four"
    ]
    []
#+end_src

Paragraphs can close off list items, so we can mix ordered and unordered list
items at the same level, as long as the differing list type is closed off by a
paragraph. In the test below, the ~Two~ closes off the unordered list, so we're
allowed to start a new ordered list with ~1) a~.

#+name: ti:0009-paragraph-can-separate-mixed-lists
#+header: :tangle test/Lilac/ParseSpec/0009-paragraph-can-separate-mixed-lists
#+begin_src org
  1) One

       - Bar

           - Baz

     Two

       1) a

       2) b

     Three

Four
#+end_src

#+name: t:0009-paragraph-can-separate-mixed-lists
#+begin_src haskell
shouldParseWith
  "0009-paragraph-can-separate-mixed-lists"
  $ nilOrgDoc
    [ CListItem
        [ LOrderedStart,
          LItemStart
        ],
      plainParagraph "One",
      CListItem
        [ LUnorderedStart,
          LItemStart
        ],
      plainParagraph "Bar",
      CListItem
        [ LUnorderedStart,
          LItemStart
        ],
      plainParagraph "Baz",
      CListItem
        [ LItemEnd,
          LUnorderedEnd,
          LItemEnd,
          LUnorderedEnd
        ],
      plainParagraph "Two",
      CListItem
        [ LOrderedStart,
          LItemStart
        ],
      plainParagraph "a",
      CListItem
        [ LItemEnd,
          LItemStart
        ],
      plainParagraph "b",
      CListItem
        [ LItemEnd,
          LOrderedEnd
        ],
      plainParagraph "Three",
      CListItem
        [ LItemEnd,
          LOrderedEnd
        ],
      plainParagraph "Four"
    ]
    []
#+end_src

It's an error if there is no separation between list types (if we suddenly try
to change list types at the same level). In the test below, the ~1) c~ is
unexpected because the unordered list has not been closed off yet.

#+name: ti:0009-lists-cannot-be-mixed-on-their-own
#+header: :tangle test/Lilac/ParseSpec/0009-lists-cannot-be-mixed-on-their-own
#+begin_src org
  - a

      - b

  1) c
#+end_src

#+name: t:0009-lists-cannot-be-mixed-on-their-own
#+begin_src haskell
shouldFailWithMessage
  "0009-lists-cannot-be-mixed-on-their-own"
  "expected UNORDERED list item at this level"
  23
#+end_src

The same applies for going the other way (trying to fit an unordered list into
an ordered one at the same level).

#+name: ti:0009-lists-cannot-be-mixed-on-their-own-ordered
#+header: :tangle test/Lilac/ParseSpec/0009-lists-cannot-be-mixed-on-their-own-ordered
#+begin_src org
  1) a

       1) b

  - c
#+end_src

#+name: t:0009-lists-cannot-be-mixed-on-their-own-ordered
#+begin_src haskell
shouldFailWithMessage
  "0009-lists-cannot-be-mixed-on-their-own-ordered"
  "expected ORDERED list item at this level"
  25
#+end_src

List items have to be precisely indented; otherwise they'll hit an indentation
error. In the test below, the ~1)~ for the ~b~ item has only 4 spaces of
indentation instead of 5 (to line up with the ~a~ above it), so that should
trigger an indentation error.

#+name: ti:0009-lists-require-precise-indentation
#+header: :tangle test/Lilac/ParseSpec/0009-lists-require-precise-indentation
#+begin_src org
  1) a

    1) b
#+end_src

#+name: t:0009-lists-require-precise-indentation
#+begin_src haskell
shouldFailWithMessage
  "0009-lists-require-precise-indentation"
  "list item: invalid indentation"
  15
#+end_src

List items must be separated by at least one blank line. In the test case below,
there is not enough space so the ~1) b~ gets interpreted as regular paragraph
text, as part of the ~a~ above it.

#+name: ti:0009-list-items-need-blank-line-separator
#+header: :tangle test/Lilac/ParseSpec/0009-list-items-need-blank-line-separator
#+begin_src org
  1) a
     1) b
#+end_src

#+name: t:0009-list-items-need-blank-line-separator
#+begin_src haskell
shouldParseWith
  "0009-list-items-need-blank-line-separator"
  $ nilOrgDoc
    [ CListItem
        [ LOrderedStart,
          LItemStart
        ],
      plainParagraph "a 1) b",
      CListItem
        [ LItemEnd,
          LOrderedEnd
        ]
    ]
    []
#+end_src

Ordered list items at the same level must increment by 1 each time. In the test
case below, the ~1) b~ will fail to parse because it should be ~2) b~ instead.

#+name: ti:0009-ordered-lists-require-increment
#+header: :tangle test/Lilac/ParseSpec/0009-ordered-lists-require-increment
#+begin_src org
  1) a

  1) b
#+end_src

#+name: t:0009-ordered-lists-require-increment
#+begin_src haskell
shouldFailWithMessage
  "0009-ordered-lists-require-increment"
  "expected incremented list head 2 but got 1"
  13
#+end_src

Ordered list items must start from 1.

#+name: ti:0009-ordered-lists-start-from-1
#+header: :tangle test/Lilac/ParseSpec/0009-ordered-lists-start-from-1
#+begin_src org
  1) a

       20) b
#+end_src

#+name: t:0009-ordered-lists-start-from-1
#+begin_src haskell
shouldFailWithMessage
  "0009-ordered-lists-start-from-1"
  "starting list head number must be 1; got 20"
  19
#+end_src

* Description lists, aka glossary terms
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       73acf466-6941-4c3b-9d6d-e0843d9b0480
:END:

[[https://orgmode.org/manual/Plain-Lists.html][Description lists]] are unordered list items that have a term followed by two
colons (~::~), and a prose description of that term. Structurally they are quite
similar to footnote definitions, which also have a leading ~name~ followed by the
~body~ (see [[data:Footnote]]).

The term "description list" comes from [[https://developer.mozilla.org/en-US/docs/Web/HTML/Reference/Elements/dl][HTML]], and it would appear that Orgmode
borrowed the idea quite directly. For example, vanilla Orgmode will export the
following Org syntax

#+begin_src org
  - foo /hello/ :: bar

  - baz :: quux
#+end_src

to HTML as

#+begin_src html
<dl class="org-dl">
<dt>foo <i>hello</i></dt><dd>bar</dd>

<dt>baz</dt><dd>quux</dd>
</dl>
#+end_src

In HTML it is perfectly valid to have multiple ~<dt>~ (term) elements, or multiple
~<dd>~ (definition) elements, or both.

There are three differences worth noting for Lilac:

   1) Only one term and one description is allowed. If multiple terms mean the
      same thing, there should be multiple entries and all but one should
      redirect the user to look up the canonical entry that has the full
      definition. Multiple definitions for a single term are not allowed.
      Instead, you should use terms like ~foo (1) :: ...~ and ~foo (2) :: ...~ to
      define two different definitions for the same term.

   2) We don't call these description lists because that language is
      too close to the implementation detail. Instead we call them "glossary
      terms" (or simply "terms") because that's what they are used for most of
      the time.

   3) Lilac treats each key-value pair as a single [[data:Cell]], so you can write
      them anywhere a cell is expected. So whereas Orgmode only allows glossary
      terms them inside unordered lists, in Lilac you can write them as their
      own independent paragraphs, or in ordered lists, etc.

      This way Lilac can mix and match glossary terms with other items (it does
      not need the [[https://emacs.stackexchange.com/questions/17244/org-mode-list-with-a-mix-of-unordered-and-description-list-items][Orgmode workarounds described here]]). See [[id:eee95795-b51b-4de5-b39d-8dda436da451][how glossary terms
      are woven]] to see how this plays out during HTML export.

With those things out of the way, let's look at [[data:GlossaryTerm]], our take on
definition list entries:

#+name: data:GlossaryTerm
#+begin_src haskell
type GlossaryTerm :: Type
data GlossaryTerm = GlossaryTerm
  { term :: Prose,
    definition :: Prose
  }
  deriving stock (Eq, Ord, Generic, Show)
  deriving anyclass (FromJSON, ToJSON)
#+end_src

Glossary terms are created from [[newtype:Prose]] in [[f:convertToGlossaryTerm]]. A
paragraph is checked if it looks like a glossary term entry in
[[f:paragraphParser]], and delegates to [[f:convertToGlossaryTerm]] if so.

#+name: f:convertToGlossaryTerm
#+begin_src haskell
convertToGlossaryTerm :: Prose -> Int -> OrgParser Cell
convertToGlossaryTerm prose offset =
  toGlossaryTerm
    $ foldl' f ([], Prose [], []) prose.unProse
  where
    f (termPTokens, term'@(Prose finalTermPTokens), def) pToken
      | finalTermPTokens == [] =
          if isGlossaryTermDelimiter pToken -- (ref:f:convertToGlossaryTerm-isGlossaryTermDelimiter)
            then
              ( termPTokens,
                Prose . reverse $ dropWhitespacePToken termPTokens,
                def
              )
            else (pToken : termPTokens, term', def)
      | otherwise = (termPTokens, term', pToken : def)
    dropWhitespacePToken :: [PToken] -> [PToken] -- (ref:dropWhitespacePToken)
    dropWhitespacePToken [] = []
    dropWhitespacePToken tokens@(pToken : rest)
      | (PTokenStyledText styledText) <- pToken,
        Just (_, ' ') <- T.unsnoc styledText.body =
          rest
      | otherwise = tokens
    toGlossaryTerm :: ([PToken], Prose, [PToken]) -> OrgParser Cell
    toGlossaryTerm (_, term', def) = do
      let proseDefinition = compressProse . Prose $ reverse def
      assertUniqueGlossaryTerm (toText term') offset -- (ref:f:convertToGlossaryTerm-assertUniqueGlossaryTerm)
      pure
        $ CGlossaryTerm
          GlossaryTerm
            { term = term',
              definition = proseDefinition
            }
#+end_src

[[(dropWhitespacePToken)]] removes any whitespace token just before the ~::~
delimiter. This is needed because otherwise the ~term~ string will end with
trailing whitespace.

[[f:convertToGlossaryTerm]] just checks each ~PToken~ in ~prose~ if it looks like a
glossary term delimiter via [[f:isGlossaryTermDelimiter]]
[[(f:convertToGlossaryTerm-isGlossaryTermDelimiter)]]. We have to check each ~PToken~
because we cannot tell what the term looks like; for example it could be that
the term is 15 words long before we finally see the ~::~ delimiter.

#+name: f:isGlossaryTermDelimiter
#+begin_src haskell
isGlossaryTermDelimiter :: PToken -> Bool
isGlossaryTermDelimiter = \case
  PTokenStyledText styledText ->
    styledText.body == "::" && maskToStyles styledText.style == []
  _ -> False
#+end_src

The important bit here is the check of whether no styles are on when
encountering the string ~::~. This way, we don't interpret strings like

#+begin_src org
Foo ~a::b~ bar.
#+end_src

as a glossary term because the ~::~ will be inside the code (~\~~) delimiters.

We require all glossary terms to be unique within the document in
[[f:assertUniqueGlossaryTerm]] [[(f:convertToGlossaryTerm-assertUniqueGlossaryTerm)]].

#+name: f:assertUniqueGlossaryTerm
#+begin_src haskell
assertUniqueGlossaryTerm :: Text -> Int -> OrgParser ()
assertUniqueGlossaryTerm term offset = do
  pState <- get
  when (member term pState.glossary) $ do
    MP.setOffset offset -- (ref:glossary-term-offset)
    fail -- (ref:fail-because-duplicate-term)
      $ "glossary term "
      <> show term
      <> " is not unique"
  put
    pState
      { OrgParserState.glossary =
          insert
            term
            pState.cellNumber
            pState.glossary
      }
#+end_src

#+name: ti:0015-glossary-duplicate-term
#+header: :tangle test/Lilac/ParseSpec/0015-glossary-duplicate-term
#+begin_src org
foo :: 1

foo :: 2
#+end_src

#+name: t:0015-glossary-duplicate-term
#+begin_src haskell
shouldFailWithMessage
  "0015-glossary-duplicate-term"
  "glossary term \"foo\" is not unique"
  14
#+end_src

Originally we naively set the [[(glossary-term-offset)]] to be the beginning of the
paragraph (as seen by [[f:paragraphParser]]). However, this had the side effect of
making Megaparsec think that we had not consumed any input when failing at
[[(fail-because-duplicate-term)]]. So then Megaparsec would also report other parser
errors for those parsers that also did not consume any input and still signaled
a failure in [[f:cellParser]], namely in [[f:listItemsEndParser]]
([[(list-items-end-failure-with-no-input)]]). In order to suppress that error, we
changed [[f:textParser]] to set a ~glossaryTermOffset~ if it detected the glossary
term delimiter ([[(glossary-term-delimiter-detection)]]) [fn:megaparsec-errors].

** Printing glossaries
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       e8ad30e5-5305-40e7-80ed-78bcec636ec1
:END:

Similar to [[id:e24de101-bf1d-418b-a18f-b1a98fea9696][Printing bibliographies]], we would like to allow users to print a
collection of all glossary terms found in the document (with links back to where
the term was originally defined --- for added context). We use the line
~#+print_glossary: local~ for this. The ~local~ just means printing the glossary
terms found in the current document. You can change this to be ~global~ to print
all glossary terms found in the entire [[id:25827c17-2015-434b-b686-db3d72b2ee4d::data:Archive][data:Archive]] (e.g., see [[id:a84a785d-5910-473a-8995-1fbfa5ea05d0][Glossary
(Global)]]).

#+name: f:glossaryLocationParser
#+begin_src haskell
glossaryLocationParser :: OrgParser Cell
glossaryLocationParser = do
  _ <- string "#+print_glossary: "
  isGlobal <-
    MP.choice
      [ string "local" >> pure False,
        string "global" >> pure True
      ]
  pure . CCommand $ PrintGlossary isGlobal
#+end_src

#+name: ti:0015-glossary-location
#+header: :tangle test/Lilac/ParseSpec/0015-glossary-location
#+begin_src org
,#+print_glossary: local
#+end_src

#+name: t:0015-glossary-location
#+begin_src haskell
shouldParseWithState
  "0015-glossary-location"
  OrgDoc
    { oid = nilOid,
      cells = [CCommand $ PrintGlossary False],
      pseudoEdges = [],
      footnoteRefs = M.empty,
      glossary = M.empty,
      lineLabels = M.empty,
      cslPath = Nothing,
      bibPaths = [],
      citations = [],
      meta =
        fromList
          [ ("ID", "00000000-0000-0000-0000-000000000000")
          ]
    }
  defaultOrgParserState
#+end_src

#+name: ti:0015-glossary-location-global
#+header: :tangle test/Lilac/ParseSpec/0015-glossary-location-global
#+begin_src org
,#+print_glossary: global
#+end_src

#+name: t:0015-glossary-location-global
#+begin_src haskell
shouldParseWithState
  "0015-glossary-location-global"
  OrgDoc
    { oid = nilOid,
      cells = [CCommand $ PrintGlossary True],
      pseudoEdges = [],
      footnoteRefs = M.empty,
      glossary = M.empty,
      lineLabels = M.empty,
      cslPath = Nothing,
      bibPaths = [],
      citations = [],
      meta =
        fromList
          [ ("ID", "00000000-0000-0000-0000-000000000000")
          ]
    }
  defaultOrgParserState
#+end_src

* Figures
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       a090bf9c-0e52-422b-b0f0-51e3b1bf1698
:END:

A [[data:Figure][Figure]] is just a link to an image file, preceded by some metadata.

#+name: data:Figure
#+begin_src haskell
type Figure :: Type
data Figure = Figure
  { parentOid :: OID, -- (ref:data:Figure-field-parentOid)
    meta :: Map Text Text, -- (ref:data:Figure-field-meta)
    caption :: Prose, -- (ref:data:Figure-field-caption)
    link :: Link -- (ref:data:Figure-field-link)
  }
  deriving stock (Eq, Ord, Generic, Show)
  deriving anyclass (FromJSON, ToJSON)
#+end_src

The ~parentOid~ [[(data:Figure-field-parentOid)]] is just like the one in [[data:Block]].

The metadata (~meta~ [[(data:Figure-field-meta)]]) recognizes certain reserved keys:

  - type :: (Required) Used to force there to be at least 1 key/value pair in
    the metadata. Otherwise when the parser comes across a link to an image on
    its own without metadata, it is ambiguous whether the link is part of a
    1-link paragraph cell, or as part of a figure cell.

  - name :: (Optional) Used to name the cell uniquely in the surrounding Org
    file. Other cells can refer to this figure by this name (or as part of a
    global [[id:f8254d58-7a3d-4595-a265-e0d6255e6ab5::data:LinkOid][data:LinkOid]]). The name is optional because all figures, named or
    not, are always assigned a number like ~Figure 1~ or ~Figure 12~.

  - caption :: (Optional) Displayed as the caption for the image. If there is no
    caption, on weaving the only name of the figure will be just the figure
    number, like ~Figure 1~ or ~Figure 12~. You can use styled text (*bold*, etc),
    links, and inline math, just like regular paragraphs (the only caveat is
    that you are not allowed to have newlines).

  - width :: (Optional) Width of the image (as a percentage of page width or
    pixel count), for HTML weaving.

  - height :: (Optional) Height of the image (as a percentage of page width or
    pixel count), for HTML weaving.

The caption (taken from the metadata) is saved into the ~caption~ field
[[(data:Figure-field-caption)]]. Finally the ~link~ [[(data:Figure-field-link)]] is the
link to the image which will be displayed in the woven HTML.

Below is an example of a figure:

#+name: fig:PNG image
#+caption: [[https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/PNG][Portable Network Graphics]] sample image.
#+type: figure
#+width: 50%
#+height: 40
[[file:image/PNG-Gradient.png]]

Below is another example, but the figure does not have a ~name~ in the metadata,
nor a ~caption~. This is still valid.

#+type: figure
#+width: 50%
#+height: 40
[[file:image/PNG-Gradient.png]]

The only practical difference is that the unnamed figure is not able to be
referenced using a [[id:f8254d58-7a3d-4595-a265-e0d6255e6ab5::data:LinkOid][data:LinkOid]] by this or other Org files.

[[f:figureParser]] is responsible for parsing figures.

#+name: f:figureParser
#+begin_src haskell
figureParser :: OrgParser Cell
figureParser = do
  pState <- get
  when (pState.listLevels /= [])
    $ fail "figures are not allowed inside list items"
  _ <-
    MP.try
      $ MP.lookAhead
        ( metaParser
            >> fileLinkParser
            >> endOfCell -- (ref:f:figureParser-endOfCell)
        )
  metaKVs <- metaParser -- (ref:f:figureParser-metadata)
  when (lookup "type" metaKVs /= Just "figure")
    $ fail "type must be 'figure'"
  caption <- case lookup "caption" metaKVs of -- (ref:f:figureParser-caption)
    Nothing -> pure $ Prose []
    Just s -> case MP.runParser (runStateT captionParser pState) "" s of
      Left err -> fail $ MP.errorBundlePretty err
      Right (prose, _pState) -> pure prose
  link <- fileLinkParser -- (ref:f:figureParser-link)
  let newFigureNumber = pState.figureNumber + 1
      figureNumberMeta = ("__figure_number", T.show newFigureNumber)
  case link.target of
    LinkTargetOid {}
    LinkTargetLineLabel {}
    LinkTargetURI {}
    LinkTargetFootnote {} ->
        fail "'file:...' required for link to figures" -- (ref:f:figureParser-file-link-requirement)
    LinkTargetFilePath _ -> do
      modify (\s -> s {figureNumber = newFigureNumber})
      pure
        . CFigure
        $ Figure
          { parentOid =
              firstParentOid pState.docOid pState.headingOidStack,
            meta =
              foldl'
                (\acc (k, v) -> insert k v acc)
                (delete "type" metaKVs)
                [figureNumberMeta],
            caption = caption,
            link = link
          }
  where
    endOfCell :: OrgParser () -- (ref:f:figureParser-endOfCell-definition)
    endOfCell =
      MP.choice
        [ void . MP.try $ string "\n\n",
          char '\n' >> MP.eof
        ]
#+end_src

First we have to parse for the metadata [[(f:figureParser-metadata)]], then the
optional caption [[(f:figureParser-caption)]], and then the link to the image
[[(f:figureParser-link)]]. For simplicity, we don't allow figures inside list items.
For the link target itself, we check that it's pointing to a file (instead of
accepting just any link) [[(f:figureParser-file-link-requirement)]].

It's important that we expect the end of the cell [[(f:figureParser-endOfCell)]]
[[(f:figureParser-endOfCell-definition)]], because otherwise we'll end up parsing
the link at the start of a paragraph cell as a figure.

** Tests

Below we parse a doc that has figures and blocks, both with metadata.

#+name: ti:0011-figures-and-blocks
#+header: :tangle test/Lilac/ParseSpec/0011-figures-and-blocks
#+begin_src org
,#+name: f:f
,#+header: :xxx bar
,#+begin_src haskell
f = 1 + 1
,#+end_src

,#+name: fig:hello
,#+caption: =hello=
,#+type: figure
,#+width: 100%
[[file:image/hello.png]]

,#+name: f:g
,#+header: :yyy bar
,#+begin_src haskell
g x = x + 1
,#+end_src

,#+type: figure
[[file:image/world.png]]

  - [[file:not-figure]]

[[file:not-a-figure-2]]

[[id:00000000-0000-0000-0000-000000000000::not a figure]]

,#+type: figure
[[file:foo.png]]
#+end_src

#+name: t:0011-figures-and-blocks
#+begin_src haskell
shouldParseWithState
  "0011-figures-and-blocks"
  ( nilOrgDoc
      [ CBlock
          Block
            { parentOid = nilOid,
              meta =
                fromList
                  [ ("__block_type", "src"),
                    ("__src_language", "haskell"),
                    ("name", "f:f"),
                    ("xxx", "bar")
                  ],
              body = [BTokenString "f = 1 + 1"]
            },
        CFigure
          Figure
            { parentOid = nilOid,
              meta =
                fromList
                  [ ("__figure_number", "1"),
                    ("width", "100%"),
                    ("name", "fig:hello"),
                    ("caption", "=hello=")
                  ],
              caption =
                Prose
                  [ ptext "hello" [Verbatim]
                  ],
              link =
                Link
                  { target = LinkTargetFilePath "image/hello.png",
                    name = [stext "image/hello.png" []]
                  }
            },
        CBlock
          Block
            { parentOid = nilOid,
              meta =
                fromList
                  [ ("__block_type", "src"),
                    ("__src_language", "haskell"),
                    ("name", "f:g"),
                    ("yyy", "bar")
                  ],
              body = [BTokenString "g x = x + 1"]
            },
        CFigure
          Figure
            { parentOid = nilOid,
              meta =
                fromList
                  [ ("__figure_number", "2")
                  ],
              caption = Prose [],
              link =
                Link
                  { target = LinkTargetFilePath "image/world.png",
                    name = [stext "image/world.png" []]
                  }
            },
        CListItem
          [ LUnorderedStart,
            LItemStart
          ],
        CParagraph
          $ Prose
            [ PTokenLink
                Link
                  { target = LinkTargetFilePath "not-figure",
                    name = [stext "not-figure" []]
                  }
            ],
        CListItem
          [ LItemEnd,
            LUnorderedEnd
          ],
        CParagraph
          $ Prose
            [ PTokenLink
                Link
                  { target = LinkTargetFilePath "not-a-figure-2",
                    name = [stext "not-a-figure-2" []]
                  }
            ],
        CParagraph
          $ Prose
            [ PTokenLink
                Link
                  { target =
                      LinkTargetOid
                        LinkOid
                          { oid = nilOid,
                            name = "not a figure"
                          },
                    name = [stext "not a figure" []]
                  }
            ],
        CFigure
          Figure
            { parentOid = nilOid,
              meta =
                fromList
                  [ ("__figure_number", "3")
                  ],
              caption = Prose [],
              link =
                Link
                  { target = LinkTargetFilePath "foo.png",
                    name = [stext "foo.png" []]
                  }
            }
      ]
      [PseudoEdge (LCI 8, LinkOid {oid = fromWords 0 0, name = "not a figure"})]
  )
  defaultOrgParserState
    { cellNames =
        Set.fromList
          [ "f:f",
            "f:g",
            "fig:hello"
          ],
      figureNumber = 3
    }
#+end_src

Metadata keys that start with a double underscore are not allowed, because
Lilac expects to use them for itself internally.

#+name: ti:0016-metadata-reserved-underscores
#+header: :tangle test/Lilac/ParseSpec/0016-metadata-reserved-underscores
#+begin_src org
,#+__block_type: example
,#+begin_src haskell
f = 1 + 1
,#+end_src
#+end_src

#+name: t:0016-metadata-reserved-underscores
#+begin_src haskell
shouldFailWithMessage
  "0016-metadata-reserved-underscores"
  "cannot use leading double underscores for metadata key: \"__block_type\""
  2
#+end_src

* Tables
:PROPERTIES:
:ID:       d0f60631-8b30-4ae1-992d-49c7b0ea38fb
:END:

Tables are like [[data:Figure][figures]] in that they have some metadata. Unlike a figure which
is just a link, a table has a two-dimensional grid with some information in each
row/column. The requirement is that the first non-whitespace character starts
with a vertical bar ~|~, also known as the pipe character (for piping shell
commands together in UNIX).

Like figures, all tables are numbered. A table can also have a caption
[[(data:Table-caption)]] (just like a [[data:Figure]]).

#+name: data:Table
#+begin_src haskell
type Table :: Type
data Table = Table
  { parentOid :: OID, -- (ref:data:Table-parentOid)
    meta :: Map Text Text,
    caption :: Prose, -- (ref:data:Table-caption)
    grid :: Map (Int, Int) Prose -- (ref:data:Table-grid)
  }
  deriving stock (Eq, Ord, Generic, Show)
  deriving anyclass (FromJSON, ToJSON)
#+end_src

The ~parentOid~ [[(data:Table-parentOid)]] is just like the one in [[data:Block]].

Below is an example of a table:

#+begin_src org
,#+name: tbl:Programming languages
,#+caption: Programming languages and typing disciplines.
| Language | Typing          |
|----------+-----------------|
| LISP     | dynamic, strong |
| C        | static, weak    |
| Haskell  | static, strong  |
#+end_src

which is rendered in HTML as

#+name: tbl:Programming languages
#+caption: Programming languages and typing disciplines.
| Language | Typing          |
|----------+-----------------|
| LISP     | dynamic, strong |
| C        | static, weak    |
| Haskell  | static, strong  |

Because we named the above with a ~#+name~ keyword, we can refer to it as
~[[tbl:Programming languages]]~ ([[tbl:Programming languages]]).

The [[f:tableParser]] parses tables.

#+name: f:tableParser
#+begin_src haskell
tableParser :: OrgParser Cell
tableParser = do
  pState <- get
  _ <- MP.try $ MP.lookAhead (metaParser >> tableRowParser)
  metaKVs <- metaParser
  caption <- case lookup "caption" metaKVs of
    Nothing -> pure $ Prose []
    Just s -> case MP.runParser (runStateT captionParser pState) "" s of -- (ref:f:tableParser-MP.runParser)
      Left err -> fail $ MP.errorBundlePretty err
      Right (prose, _pState) -> pure prose
  rows <- MP.some $ tableLineParser <|> tableRowParser
  _ <- MP.optional tableFormulaParser -- (ref:f:tableParser-tableFormulaParser)
  let newTableNumber = pState.tableNumber + 1
  modify (\s -> s {tableNumber = newTableNumber})
  pure
    . CTable
    $ Table
      { parentOid =
          firstParentOid pState.docOid pState.headingOidStack,
        meta = insert "__table_number" (T.show newTableNumber) metaKVs,
        caption = caption,
        grid = fromList $ rowsToTuples rows -- (ref:f:tableParser-rowsToTuples)
      }
#+end_src

The [[f:captionParser]] parses the caption text from a ~#+caption: ...~ into prose.
This way the caption can support other text styles, links, and even math
fragments. We parse the text again by invoking ~MP.runParser~
[[(f:tableParser-MP.runParser)]], because it's easy to implement; a fancier
alternative would be to enrich the [[f:metaParser]] to hold values that can be
either raw text or prose.

#+name: f:captionParser
#+begin_src haskell
captionParser :: OrgParser Prose
captionParser = do
  caption <- proseParser $ whitespaceCompressor onlySpaces
  failIfLeftoverActiveStyles
  pure caption
#+end_src

The [[f:tableLineParser]] skips over horizontal rules (like ~|---+-----|~) that
typically separate the table heading (column names) from the rest of the rows
that hold the actual data. It also skips over any leading indentation in the
subsequent line, based on the ~continuationIndent~ which is set by
[[f:listHeadIndentLevelParser]]. This way, we can parse tables inside list items.

#+name: f:tableLineParser
#+begin_src haskell
tableLineParser :: OrgParser [Prose]
